Each player's row is {left:[], center:[], right:[]}. center holds exactly one card. The first card either player plays in a match always lands in center, regardless of which side (left/right) was requested — subsequent cards go to whichever flank was actually chosen. If a card dies out of center and a flank has cards, the nearest flank card collapses into center automatically (a 50/50 coin flip when both flanks are non-empty) — this is the collapseIn event.

Columns are numbered symmetrically per side, both sides sharing one numbering scheme, with center as column 0. combatColumnsOf() is the one function that derives a card's column from its array position — never infer column position any other way.

Turn order

Attackers are gathered (gatherAttackers: Wait=0, not Reloading/Stunned/Shell-skipped), then sorted by Quick-keyword first, then by absolute board column left-to-right across BOTH sides (sharing one column numbering), falling back to side (enemy before player) only when two attackers share a column. This means play visibly alternates top/bottom as it goes left to right, not "resolve one whole side, then the other." Which side wins a same-column tie alternates by round (not hardcoded to one side).

Targeting

An attacker's primary target uses the rank-interlock/parity facing rule (resolveLiveTarget): both sides' whole rows are centered as one unit and matched by position, so unequal row counts correctly interlock instead of just comparing raw column numbers — a card can end up facing one direct opponent, two straddled opponents (50/50 pick), one opponent-or-castle (50/50), or the castle outright, depending on parity. If there's no live opponent to resolve to, the attack goes straight to the enemy HQ. Stealth overrides targeting entirely and always hits the HQ directly, ignoring column state.

Sweep and Swipe are both "extra hit" keywords that key off the primary target's resolved column, but they are NOT the same mechanic:

  • Sweep (effects.sweep = N, a number) continues further down the SAME flank the attacker is facing, past the primary target, for N additional live cards (findNextLiveCardBeyond). It never touches the castle — if it runs off the end of that flank, the extra hits just don't happen.

  • Swipe (effects.swipe = true, a boolean — redesigned 2026-09-21) hits the two enemy columns immediately flanking the primary target's own column (target.col-1 and target.col+1), regardless of which flank the attacker itself is on. A live card in one of those columns takes a full hit; an empty/off-board flank column redirects that swing straight to the enemy castle instead (no Siege bonus — Siege is reserved for the PRIMARY hit landing on the castle). So a lone defender with nothing on either side of it eats the castle hit twice — once per empty flank. Swipe only fires when the primary attack itself landed on a card (there's no reference column to flank from if the primary hit already resolved straight to HQ).

Both Sweep's continuation hits and Swipe's flank hits get their own independent Evasion roll, Guardian redirect, and full per-hit status treatment (poison/bleed/scar/Chained-break/Sap/kill- credit/onAttacked) — same as a primary hit, just resolved a second (or third) time.

Resolution order (one full round, synchronous in the engine)

Round-start custom triggers → poison ticks (deliberately BEFORE Renewal/Regen/Round-Start/Per- Turn, so a Renewed card still eats one guaranteed tick) → gather attackers → sort (Quick, then column, side breaks ties) → normal attacks (Stealth override; primary hit always connects; Guardian may redirect; Sweep/Swipe extra hits are each independently Evasion-gated; thorns reflection; onAttackedSpawn; Shell/Expose/poison queued) → death cleanup (bounty gold, onDeathSpawn, exile-on-death, onKill triggers) → end-of-round upkeep (Shell activate/deactivate, Reload cooldown, Stun clears, Wait decrements once, onReadyDamage/missile(onReady)/render(onReady) fire, onReadyGold/onReadyPrayer pay out) → death cleanup again → game-over check.

Important: the engine runs an ENTIRE round synchronously, including removing dead cards, before any of it is animated client-side. The UI replays the event log afterward as a paced sequence. This is why matchState.replayCards (hp/status snapshot) and matchState.replayRows (row-membership snapshot) exist — a card that died mid-round is already spliced out of the live board array by the time its OWN earlier-in-the-round events get replayed, so the UI can't just query the live board for it. Any new per-event UI logic that needs a card's state mid-replay should read from these snapshots, not the live board, unless it's a freshly-spawned uid the snapshot doesn't know about yet (live board is the correct fallback there).

Trigger/effect vocabulary

Triggers: onSpawn, onReady (fires once, the round Wait first hits 0 — not every round), onDeath, onAttack, onAttacked, onKill (melee kills only, not ranged/onReadyDamage), onExile, onAllySpawn, onRoundStart. Actions: damage, gain gold/prayer, draw, spawn, buff/ debuff attack or health, poison, expose, stun, reduce-wait-of-spawned-ally, exile self. The Codex's Reference tab is generated from the same registry the editor's dropdowns use, so it can't drift from what's actually implemented.

Passive keywords currently implemented: Armor, Thorns, Swipe, Sweep, Rage, Expose, Guardian, Quick, Reload, Shell, Stealth, Evasion/Evade, Bleed, Fester, Rupture, Rally, Esprit, Bloodlust, Berserker, Regenerate, Chained, Resist, Weakness, Scurry, and more — see game-design.md's per-version sections for what each does; this doc doesn't re-derive every one since the Codex Reference tab is the always-current source of truth for their exact current behavior.

Known open design questions (do not guess at these — ask first)

  • Column facing when flank counts mismatch (a lane with more cards on one side than the other) — largely addressed by the rank-interlock/parity facing rule (resolveLiveTarget), but see game-design-action-items.md, Category: Combat rules for any remaining edge cases.

  • Field "rebalancing" to recenter an uneven flank spread — a bigger change than it sounds, since several mechanics (Guardian adjacency, Thorns, sweep-continuation) lean on today's flank-array semantics. See the same doc.

  • Evade/Evasion merge — two genuinely different dodge maths (alternating vs. probabilistic), not just two names for one mechanic. Needs a decision on which semantics survive.

claude/context-economy-progression.md read before: currencies, rewards, levels

Context: Economy & Progression

Read this before touching costs, rewards, currencies, or the leveling/Forge system. Update it in place (not as a changelog) when any of this changes.

Two separate currency systems — do not conflate them

In-match resources (exist only during a live match, reset every game): Gold (pl.acorns field name — a historical artifact, not a typo), Prayer, Devilry, and Stone (added later as a fourth). These gate playing cards (cost, prayerCost, devilryCost, exile costs) and are generated by onSpawnGold/onReadyGold/onSpawnPrayer/onReadyPrayer effects, bounty kills, and discarding a hand card for gold (max once per turn, human only). Shown in the in-match HUD.

Out-of-match currencies (persist across matches, localStorage-backed via loadCurrencies()/saveCurrencies()): Gold, Gems, Magic Dust. These power card leveling (below) and only ever appear on the Player/Forge tabs, never inside a live match's HUD. Naming overlap is a known, flagged issue — both systems currently use the word "Gold" for different things. The in-match resource's rename to "Stone" was proposed specifically to resolve this but is a separate, not-yet-done backlog item (see roadmap-and-backlog.md) — don't assume it's done, and don't casually rename either currency without checking that backlog item first, since a rename touches dozens of call sites (see that doc's own note on why it's not a quick tweak).

The out-of-match Gold currency displays with coinIconHTML() — a custom inline SVG coin, referencing a shared <radialGradient id="coinIcoGrad"> defined once in arena_template.html's <body> — not the 🪙 emoji, which is reserved for the in-match resource specifically so the two stay visually distinguishable. Don't swap either back to a shared glyph.

Devilry ("Dark Points") generation shapes

Devilry (pl.devilry) is an in-match resource with several ways to generate it, all landing on the same field via the same ensureStat(...).devilryGenerated bookkeeping and pushing the same {type:'devilry', ...} replay event — a new generation shape should follow this pattern rather than inventing a parallel one. As of 2026-09-21, the full set:

  • effects.onSpawnDevilry / effects.onReadyDevilry — legacy hand-authored top-level fields (mirroring Gold/Prayer's own onSpawnGold/onReadyGold shape). onReadyDevilry is a "per round while Ready" payout (gated on c.wait===0, in the same board-wide pass that also grants onReadyGold/onReadyPrayer), not a one-time "just became ready" trigger — same onReady-vs-perTurn naming trap mechanics-guideline.md already calls out for other onReady*-named hooks.

  • effects.triggers with {on:'perTurn', do:'gainDevilry', amount:N} — the generic Custom Trigger route, now the intended way to AUTHOR a new "generates Devilry every ready round" card from scratch (see test_dark_points.js and its own comment on why perTurn was corrected from an earlier, wrong onReady authoring).

  • effects.inherentDarkness (number, added 2026-09-21) — a named PASSIVE_DEFS/SKILL_DEFS convenience preset for exactly the onReadyDevilry payout above (same gate, same bookkeeping, same event shape) — lets a card carry "generates N Dark Points per round" as a plain numeric "+ Add Skill" row instead of hand-authoring the Custom Trigger. Implemented as its own if block right next to onReadyDevilry's in bramblewood-engine.js (additive if a card somehow has both, though no card is expected to).

  • The archetype-wide Devilry-line discard bonus (discardCardByUid() in arena_app.js, darkPointBonus) — any card whose inferMechanicLine(...)==='devilry' grants +1 Devilry when PITCHED (discarded for its resource yield), on top of its normal pitch yield. This is an archetype-level rule, not a per-card effect field.

  • effects.coreEvil (boolean, added 2026-09-21) — a CARD-level pitch-time bonus, +1 Devilry the moment this specific card is pitched, independent of the Devilry mechanic line entirely (a card with no Devilry archetype tag at all can still carry it) and additive with the archetype-wide bonus above if a card happens to have both (implemented as coreEvilBonus computed alongside darkPointBonus in discardCardByUid(), summed into one darkPointBonus total).

Both new passives also extend the ★ Dark-Point abadge condition (arena_app.js, the e.onSpawnDevilry||e.onReadyDevilry||... check) — any future Devilry-generation shape should be added to that same condition, or a card carrying it will show no ★ badge at all despite genuinely granting Dark Points.

Card leveling (0→10)

Every card has an independent level 0-10, stored in myCardLevels (localStorage-backed via loadCardLevels()/saveCardLevels()), keyed by defId. Leveling up costs Magic Dust + a smaller amount of Gold, rising per level. The stat curve is 1 + 0.06L + 0.004L² (exactly +100% at level 10), floored at base + level so a low-stat card's early levels are never rounded away to nothing by the multiplier. Leveling does NOT change a card's rarity band or visual rarity treatment — it's shown as a small level badge instead, deliberately kept independent of the rarity system.

getCardDefs() is the one choke point that applies leveling to a card's live stats — anywhere in the UI that reads a card's attack/health for display should go through it (or the board's own live instance data, for a card actually in play) rather than reading CARD_DEFS_BASELINE directly, or a leveled card's boost won't show up.

Known open limitation: stats are looked up per-defId in one shared table — a leveled card's boost is NOT yet scoped away from an AI opponent who happens to field the same card. This means today, leveling YOUR Otter Centurion also makes the AI's Otter Centurion stronger if it draws one. Flagged, not silently worked around — needs a per-owner stat-scoping pass before this is fully correct.

The Forge tab

A card picker (from your unlocked roster) + a Level Up button. Clicking Level Up: deducts currency, increments the card's level, then (as of task list item 4, 2026-09-18) plays a 3D card-flip reveal (flipRevealCard() — see context-vfx-animation.md) showing old stats flipping to new stats, timed so the star-fall celebration fires right as the flip completes, before the full Forge panel re-renders (~900ms total deferred). The underlying data mutation is synchronous on click — only the visual confirmation is paced behind the flip.

Star-fall celebration

Reused across multiple moments (Forge level-ups, match wins): a burst of star glyphs that jump out from a source element and fall like leaves, reusing the Autumn Village weather system's own physics keyframes. See context-vfx-animation.md for the implementation.

Known open items

  • Gold→Stone rename (in-match resource) — proposed, not built. See roadmap-and-backlog.md for why it's scoped as its own session rather than a quick patch, and the open design question of what Lumber/Elemental Energy (if ever added) would each be FOR.

  • Leveling's per-owner stat-scoping gap (above).

  • No shop/spend loop for Gold/Gems beyond the Forge yet — flagged in roadmap-and-backlog.md Part 5 as a natural next addition once currencies were stable.

  • Deckbuilding rules discussed but not implemented anywhere: copy limits per rarity, a Leader that can't also appear as a regular deck copy, target deck size conventions beyond the flat 20-card rule that IS enforced today.

claude/context-ui-layout.md read before: screens, layout, phone sizes

Context: UI & Layout

Read this before touching CSS structure, card sizing, responsive breakpoints, or the topbar/nav. Update it in place (not as a changelog) when the layout system changes.

Card sizing

One CSS variable pair, --match-card-w / --match-card-h (140×160 desktop, 76×92 under the mobile breakpoint), consumed identically by .hq-tile (castle/deck/leader widgets), .board-card (cards actually in play), and .hand-strip .card-tile (hand). Resizing any in-match card means editing these two numbers in one place — do not reintroduce per-context hardcoded sizes for these three; that was a real, since-fixed bug (three independently-drifted sizes, see game-design-v29-addendum.md item 3).

This is a DIFFERENT, deliberately separate sizing system from the Codex/pool/deck-editor/Forge's own .card-tile default (100px, auto height) — that's a genuinely different job (browsing many cards at once) from "cards actually in play," and should stay independent.

Page structure: topbar vs. content width

#appWrap contains .topbar-wrap (the nav bar) and .content-wrap (everything below it), both independently capped/widened by CSS — this split exists specifically so the nav bar's position never shifts when switching between narrow views (Codex/Player/Arena/Forge, capped at 1220px) and wide views (Conquest/Raid/in-match, min(1900px, 98-99vw) via #appWrap.in-match/.wide-map classes reaching into .content-wrap only). Added 2026-09-18 (task list item 3) specifically because the nav bar visibly shifting width on every tab change read as disorienting. Do not let a future width-toggling class reach .topbar-wrap — only .content-wrap should ever change size based on the current view. No CSS transition on this width swap, per explicit request (a transition on a structural width change reads as sluggish, not smooth).

Settings panel positioning

.settings-wrap{position:relative} wraps the settings gear button + its popover panel specifically because .topbar itself has no position:relative of its own (it's a plain flex row) — the wrapper gives the absolutely-positioned .settings-panel a local anchor. If the settings button ever moves elsewhere in the topbar, keep this wrapper pattern rather than relying on .topbar gaining relative positioning (which could shift other absolutely-positioned topbar children).

.rarity-band (card rarity/legibility scrim)

A bottom-anchored gradient on .card-tile serving dual duty: rarity color indicator AND text legibility backing. As of 2026-09-18: 50% of the tile's height (was 76%), capped at .58 max black alpha (was .82), with a transparent 0%, transparent 30% lead-in so the top half of the card is untouched. The earlier, taller/darker version flattened Common/Starter (white/near-white rarity colors) into an indistinguishable dark-grey wash covering most of the card art — if this value ever needs tuning again, specifically re-check it against Common (#ffffff) and Starter (#efece0) rarity colors, since those are the ones a too-dark scrim breaks first (most other rarities have enough inherent saturation to still read through a darker scrim).

Board row overflow + cross-card stacking (2026-09-21)

.board-row uses overflow-y:clip; overflow-clip-margin:90px;, not overflow-y:hidden. Per explicit report ("Elements are still being cut out - in the battlefield"): the row pairs overflow-x:auto (needed for the horizontal-scroll-instead-of-wrap behavior, see the "visual 'cards forming a 2nd row' bug" entry in game-design-action-items.md) with a vertical overflow rule. Per the CSS Overflow spec, pairing overflow-x:auto with overflow-y:visible silently PROMOTES the visible axis to auto too (risking an unwanted vertical scrollbar) — so overflow-y can't just be set to visible to stop clipping normal in-play decoration (damage floats, status badges, the FIGHT sign, hover-zoom) that legitimately pokes past the row's box. overflow-y:clip sidesteps the promotion (it's not visible, so it doesn't trigger the pairing rule) while overflow-clip-margin:90px gives 90px of breathing room before anything actually gets clipped. This is the same fix shape already used once before for .speech-bubble (see that class's own CSS comment) — if a similar "element getting cut off" report comes in for a DIFFERENT scrollable container, check for this same overflow-x:auto + overflow-y:hidden pairing before assuming it's a z-index problem.

Cards do NOT get a permanent per-card z-index. The instinct for "cards should have higher layer order" is to just add z-index:1 (or similar) to the base .board-card rule — don't. Any non-auto z-index on a position:relative+ element creates a NEW, ISOLATING CSS stacking context for that element's descendants, which traps a card's own .dmg-float inside that one card's local stacking order — it can then no longer out-rank a NEIGHBORING card's badges/other elements, exactly backwards from the actual request ("the numbers should have the highest" layer order, across the whole row, not just within their own card). Resting cards are left at z-index:auto (no isolating context at all) specifically so .dmg-float's own z-index:40 (see below) can reach across sibling cards freely. A card that's actively lunging into its attack gets a temporary, animation-scoped lift instead: animateAttacker() adds .is-attacking (z-index:8) right when the lunge starts and removes it in every completion path (both GSAP timeline onComplete branches and the non-GSAP fallback's final setTimeout) — narrow enough in scope and duration that it never creates a lasting isolating context, and 8 still sits comfortably below .dmg-float's 40. Current cross-card z stack, low to high: resting .board-card (auto) < .abadge/skill badges (3) < hover-zoom (5) < .is-attacking (8) < .dmg-float (40). If a new element needs to sit in this stack, place it deliberately in this ladder rather than picking an arbitrary number.

Responsive breakpoint

One mobile breakpoint (search @media in arena_template.html) handles the phone-width layout: --match-card-w/--match-card-h drop to 76×92, .topbar-wrap/.content-wrap padding tightens, #appWrap.in-match .content-wrap goes to 100vw. When adding new layout rules that need a mobile variant, add them inside this existing media query block rather than introducing a second breakpoint.

Animation conventions

  • Nav emoji hover-spin: emoji glyphs are wrapped in <span class="tab-emoji"> specifically so the spin keyframe can target just the glyph, not the button's text label. Any new nav-style button with an emoji should follow this same wrapping convention if it wants the same hover treatment for free (just add the .tab-emoji class).

  • See context-vfx-animation.md for GSAP/entrance/particle conventions — this doc is structural CSS/layout only.

Known open items

  • Mobile text legibility at the 76×92 card size was flagged as worth a real-device pass (game-design-v29-addendum.md) — not yet done.

  • No dedicated design system doc beyond this one and the palette/type notes in game-design.md's "Visual identity" section — if a formal design-token system gets built, this doc is where it should be documented going forward.

claude/context-vfx-animation.md read before: animation and effects

Context: VFX & Animation

Read this before adding or changing any animation, particle effect, or entrance/exit behavior. Update it in place (not as a changelog) when the system changes. This code is described in the source itself as "extremely carefully-tuned... with many bugfix comments warning against structural changes" — treat that warning as real; prefer additive changes over restructuring.

The entrance/Flip system (GSAP)

renderBoard()'s newEls handling drives every card's entrance. Two paths exist: a GSAP path (used when GSAP is available, which is always in the shipped build) and a no-GSAP fallback (kept for robustness, not actively used). Flip.from() handles the "existing cards slide to their new position" reflow; new cards are explicitly excluded from what Flip touches and animated on a separate timeline instead — this split exists because Flip's onEnter/onLeave callbacks don't reliably fire for brand-new elements in this GSAP build, and because Flip was once found silently re-diffing against stale, already-discarded DOM elements when new-card detection wasn't kept separate (see game-design-action-items.md, "Landing impact" entry, for the full bug history — don't reintroduce that coupling).

pendingEntranceOrigins (a Map<uid, {x, y, rotate}>) is how a new card's entrance tween starts from a REAL on-screen point (where it came from) instead of a generic fall-from-above. Consumed once (deleted on use) so it can never leak onto a later, unrelated card reusing the same uid space. Three producers as of 2026-09-18:

  • Played from hand: set in playCardByUid, using the hand tile's own live bounding rect, keyed by the HAND uid (this one specifically survives into the board card, since placeCard preserves the hand-card's uid rather than reassigning — this is the ONE case where hand-uid == board-uid; every other producer below keys by the board card's own uid, which is often different from any hand uid involved).

  • onAttackedSpawn (e.g. Bee Drone spawning on being hit): set from the attacked card's own live position (ev.nearUid).

  • onDeathSpawn (e.g. Rot-Swollen-Carcass spawning 3 Maggot Swarmlings on death): set via lastDeathOrigin, a one-shot heuristic — the death-event handler records the dying card's on-screen center right before removal; the very next onDeathSpawn spawn event (which always immediately follows its triggering death within one round's replay) consumes it. Added 2026-09-18, task list item 2.

When an origin IS found, the entrance starts at scale:.16 (tuned down from an earlier .5) so it reads as "popping out of the spawner" rather than "a small card flying in." When no origin is recorded, the generic fall-in (scale:.4, y:-52) is used instead.

Trap to remember (a real bug hit while building item 2): a played card's HAND uid is not always the same as its final BOARD uid — placeCard()'s return doesn't reassign the hand-uid in every path, but any code that needs the definitive board uid (e.g. to anchor a speech bubble or VFX to the just-played card) should read it from the 'play' event's own uid field (events.find(ev=>ev.type==='play').uid), not the uid the play action was originally called with. pendingEntranceOrigins' hand-play producer is a documented, deliberate exception where the hand uid does carry through — don't assume that generalizes elsewhere.

renderVfxForEvent(ev)

The central dispatcher — one big function with an if(ev.type==='...') branch per engine event type (hit, hitHQ, death, spawn, heal, poisonTick, statusFx, etc.). Each branch typically: sound cue → floating text/number → element-specific animation (shake, lunge, flash). New event types should follow this same pattern rather than introducing a parallel dispatch mechanism.

Skills/VFX/SFX coverage audit (2026-09-21)

The reliable way to check "does every mechanic actually have a sound and a visual" is to cross-reference the two ground-truth lists against each other, rather than eyeballing renderVfxForEvent's if/else chain in isolation:

  • every events.push({type:'statusFx', kind:...}) call site in bramblewood-engine.js (the full set of kinds the engine can ever actually emit), against

  • every else if(ev.kind==='...') branch inside renderVfxForEvent's ev.type==='statusFx' block (the full set of kinds the front end actually handles).

Doing this the first time turned up two kinds the engine had been pushing for a while with no matching branch at all — bleed (the "Bleed opposite" custom-trigger action) and esprit (the Esprit passive's on-ally-spawn self-buff) — both fired completely silently: no sound, no float, no status refresh, the exact same class of gap as the pre-existing Scurry/ bleedTick silences found in earlier audit passes (see game-design-action-items.md). Both are now wired up:

  • kind:'bleed' → SoundKit.bleedApply() (a distinct apply tone, separate from bleedTick()'s round-tick sound, mirroring Poison's own apply-vs-tick split) + a '+N🩸' float using the new .dmg-float.bleed CSS class (arena_template.html — was missing; floats were silently falling back to the base --attack red) + an rc.bleed mirror into the replay snapshot so the mid-round status badge stays in sync.

  • kind:'esprit' → SoundKit.espritTone() (a warm ascending triangle chime, deliberately gentler than Berserk's growl or Momentum's escalating notes, since Esprit is a camaraderie effect, not a rage/kill-streak one) + a '🤝 +N⚔' float on the card that just grew — same "self-buff floats on the attacker's own tile" pattern Berserk/Momentum already use, no rc mutation needed for the same reason those two don't need one (the permanent Attack gain shows up via the normal stat display at round end).

Covered by test_bleed_esprit_statusfx.js. When adding a new custom-trigger action or passive that can push a statusFx event (or any new event type at all), run this same cross-reference before calling it done — a new kind/type with no branch fails silently, with no console error to catch it, so it's easy to ship an invisible mechanic by accident. This check is now folded into mechanics-guideline.md's and style-guideline.md's "before you ship" checklists.

Coverage audit, follow-up pass — Crit/Rend/Fester/Rupture/Frenzy (2026-09-21)

Same cross-reference method, next five keywords off the ~30-keyword list. Findings:

  • Crit had no dedicated sound/flourish at all — landing a crit doubled damage completely silently (only a small 💥 tucked into the battle-log text). Now: SoundKit.critTone() + a 💥 Crit! float via keywordCuesForHit, suppressed when ev.ambush is also true (Ambush gets its own distinct flourish and shouldn't double up with Crit's on the same hit).

  • Rend (bypasses Armor) and Fester/Rupture (bonus damage = the attacker's own poison/bleed stacks) folded silently into the plain hit-damage number, zero attribution — pixel-identical to an ordinary hit for the same final total. The engine now reports these as pure DISPLAY flags on the hit/hitHQ event (rend, festerBonus, ruptureBonus — the underlying damage math is unchanged, these only tell the VFX layer WHY the number is what it is): computeHitDamage/damageCard compute rendBypass = rend && nominalArmor>0 (only flags true when Rend actually mattered — a Rend attacker vs. an unarmored target reports rend:false, since nothing was bypassed); festerBonus/ruptureBonus are captured as named locals right where the bonus is added to atkThisRound. keywordCuesForHit reads these off the event and floats 🗡 Rend — Armor bypassed! (+SoundKit.rendTone()) / attributed +N☠/+N🩸 floats (+SoundKit.festerBite()/ruptureBite()) alongside the normal damage number. logText's 'hit' case appends matching text annotations.

  • Frenzy's bonus-attack requeue pushed no event at all — the second swing replayed as an indistinguishable ordinary hit, nothing signaling why a card acted twice. Fixed with a new standalone frenzy event type ({type:'frenzy', side, attDefId, attUid}) pushed right where the attack gets requeued (attackQueue.unshift(a), gated the same as the requeue itself — once per round, and not if the attacker already died to Thorns first) — a new branch in renderVfxForEvent floats 🔥 Frenzy! (+SoundKit.frenzyTone()) on the card via boardCardEl(ev.attUid).

  • Separately: Frenzy and Rend turned out to be real, shipped card mechanics (Ember Jackal / Pyroclast Wyrm) that were never added to the editor's PASSIVE_DEFS registry — no other card could ever be GIVEN either skill through the "+ Add Skill" UI, only the two cards that already hardcoded them in canonical/cards.json. Both are now real kind:'boolean' entries.

Covered by test_crit_rend_fester_rupture_frenzy.js (39 checks — engine-level Rend/Fester/ Rupture/Frenzy correctness plus browser-level SoundKit/keywordCuesForHit/registry checks).

Cross-card z-index (2026-09-21)

.dmg-float's z-index (and the deliberate choice NOT to give resting .board-cards their own z-index) is documented in context-ui-layout.md's "Board row overflow + cross-card stacking" section — read that before touching either .board-card's or .dmg-float's stacking, since the two are tightly coupled (a per-card isolating stacking context on .board-card would silently break .dmg-float's ability to out-rank a NEIGHBORING card, not just its own).

Skill/status VFX queuing (2026-09-21)

Skill and status VFX are never simultaneous — the replay loops in resolveRound() play one event at a time, await sleep(delayForEvent(ev, prevKind)) between each, so an effect always finishes before the next one starts. delayForEvent() picks the gap based on whether the CURRENT event is the same "kind" as the one immediately before it (tracked as lastVfxKind/lastWaitVfxKind in the two replay loops, computed via vfxKindOf(ev)):

  • Combat beats keep their own fixed, dedicated pacing regardless of neighboring kind: hit/ hitHQ/evaded (900ms, or 1260ms if ev.ranged), death (550ms), render (780ms).

  • Every other event type (the "skill/status VFX" bucket — poisonTick, bleedTick, statusFx and its many sub-kinds, heal, sap, explode, namedBuff, collapseIn, spawn, revive, waitTick/ready, ...) gets 200ms if vfxKindOf(ev) matches the previous event's kind, or 500ms if it doesn't (including the very first event in a sequence, which has no previous kind to match). This is the "bubbly, obvious, distinct" pacing rule — see style-guideline.md's "Skill VFX" section for the full design rationale; this doc is just the implementation pointer.

  • vfxKindOf(ev) returns ev.type for everything except statusFx, where it returns 'statusFx:'+ev.kind — statusFx bundles many visually/audibly distinct sub-mechanics (poison/freeze/stun/scar/blind/shock/corrode/berserk/momentum/bleed/esprit/...) under one event type, so two different ev.kinds must never be treated as "the same kind of thing" for queuing purposes.

  • Both places that replay an event log — the main combat/skill event loop and the separate Wait-timer/"next turn" replay — track their own lastVfxKind local variable and pass it into delayForEvent(); a new replay loop added later should follow the same pattern rather than calling delayForEvent(ev) with no previous-kind context (which always falls back to the 500ms "different kind" gap, safe but overly conservative for a genuinely repeating effect).

  • All of the above is still scaled by matchState.speedMult exactly like every other event delay always was (1x/1.5x/2x/3x, floored at 18ms).

Reusable utilities

  • floatText(el, text, cls) — appends a small floating text element directly into el (which must be position:relative), with randomized x-drift and a GSAP rise-fade timeline. The base pattern every other "float something above a card" utility below follows.

  • itemDropVfx(uid, glyph) — a heavier, bouncier drop for kill payouts (bounty gold).

  • starFallVfx.celebrate(el, count) — star particles that jump from el and fall like leaves, reusing the Autumn Village weather system's physics. Used by Forge level-ups and match wins.

  • flipRevealCard(mountEl, opts) (added 2026-09-18, item 4) — a real 3D X-axis card flip (perspective/transform-style:preserve-3d/rotateY), opts.frontHTML/opts.backHTML for the two faces, paired with SoundKit.cardFlip(). Generic — currently used for Forge level-up reveals, built to be reusable for any "flip to show what's underneath" moment (reward reveals, etc).

  • emojiBurstVfx.burst(originEl, {emojis, count, ...}) (added 2026-09-18, item 6) — a fireworks-style burst: emoji particles at varying sizes explode outward from a point, then fall under gravity and fade. Shares #starFallLayer with the star-fall celebration — no new DOM scaffolding. Built as infrastructure, not yet wired to a specific trigger moment — check game-design-v30-addendum.md and roadmap-and-backlog.md before adding a new call site, in case one was decided on since this was written.

  • showSpeechBubble(el, text) / maybeSpeak(uid, bank) (added 2026-09-18, item 8) — the speech framework's visual half. showSpeechBubble appends a .speech-bubble div into a board card element (same append-into-the-card pattern as floatText), fades it in/holds/fades out via GSAP. maybeSpeak(uid, bank) is the ONE entry point every trigger site should call — it looks up boardCardEl(uid), gates on SPEECH_CHANCE (0.28, so cards don't talk every single time) and a per-uid cooldown (SPEECH_COOLDOWN_MS, 2600ms, tracked in the lastSpokeAt Map), then calls showSpeechBubble + SoundKit.voiceTone(uid) together. SPEECH_BANKS (keyed by onSummon/onAttack/onDeath) is the one place to add new lines — see context-audio.md for the audio half and why the content is currently generic placeholder chatter rather than per-character writing.

SoundKit

Covered in full in context-audio.md — the two takeaways relevant here: every cue is built from tone()/noise(), and volume gating happens at that one choke point, so a new VFX's accompanying sound should always go through a named SoundKit method rather than calling Web Audio directly.

Timing/pacing notes worth remembering

  • resolveRound()'s event replay is paced per-event-type, doubled from its original speed (2x slowdown, game-design.md "Version 15") with a fast-forward control to compress it back down mid-round. As of 2026-09-21, the non-combat "skill/status VFX" portion of that pacing is also kind-aware (200ms same-kind / 500ms different-kind) rather than a flat per-type number — see "Skill/status VFX queuing" above.

  • afterPlayerAction() (called after every play, human or AI) keeps doing pacing/HUD work for a real, sometimes-multi-second while AFTER matchState.resolving itself already reads false — don't assume resolving===false means all post-round animation/side-effects have finished; a test or a chained animation that depends on something that fires at the very end of afterPlayerAction (like the speech framework's onSummon trigger) needs real wall-clock headroom past just polling resolving, confirmed via direct repro while building item 8 (see test_v44_settings_and_speech.js's own comment for the exact numbers observed).

claude/context-audio.md read before: sound

Context: Audio

Read this before touching any sound, any volume control, the settings menu, or the speech framework's voice cues. Update it in place (not as a changelog) when any of this changes.

System overview

All sound is synthesized in-browser via the Web Audio API — no external audio files, no CDN dependency, consistent with the single-file-artifact constraint. Everything lives in one IIFE, SoundKit, near the top of arena_app.js.

Two primitives everything else is built from:

  • tone(freq, dur, type, gainPeak, delay, volOverride) — a single oscillator envelope.
  • noise(dur, gainPeak) — a short filtered noise burst (explosions, impacts).

Every named cue (SoundKit.hit(), SoundKit.death(), SoundKit.gold(), ~40 more) is one or a few calls to these two primitives with different frequency/duration/type combinations. Adding a new cue means adding a new named method that calls tone/noise — never bypass them, since volume gating (below) only happens at that one choke point.

Volume model (three independent sliders)

As of 2026-09-18 (task list item 7), there are three persisted volume values, each 0..1:

  • sfxVolume (default 1, bw_sfxVolume in localStorage) — gates tone()/noise() directly. This is what almost every existing cue in SoundKit responds to.

  • voiceVolume (default 0.8, bw_voiceVolume) — gates only SoundKit.voiceTone(), the speech framework's "voice blip" (see below). Kept separate from sfxVolume deliberately, so a player can mute combat SFX but keep character chatter audible, or vice versa.

  • musicVolume (default 0.6, bw_musicVolume) — persisted, but there is no music system yet. No oscillator or audio element reads this value today. It exists so a future music track has a real, already-chosen volume to start at instead of defaulting to full blast the day one ships. The settings panel's Music slider carries a small note in the UI saying so — do not remove that note without actually wiring up music.

Exposed API (all on the SoundKit object): getSfxVolume()/setSfxVolume(v), getVoiceVolume()/setVoiceVolume(v), getMusicVolume()/setMusicVolume(v). Each setter clamps to [0,1] and persists to its own localStorage key. setSfxVolume(0) also calls stopAll() so dragging the slider to zero mid-cue cuts off immediately rather than just blocking the next one.

Back-compat only: isMuted()/setMuted(v) still exist (isMuted() ⇔ sfxVolume<=0), kept in case something outside this codebase still calls them. Nothing in this app calls them anymore — the settings panel talks to get/setSfxVolume directly. Don't build new features against isMuted/setMuted.

The settings panel

HTML lives in arena_template.html (.settings-wrap / #settingsBtn / #settingsPanel, replacing the old single mute-toggle button). Wiring is initSettingsPanel() in arena_app.js (search for that name) — a small IIFE that binds each of the three <input type="range"> sliders to its matching getter/setter pair, syncs the displayed percentage, and handles open/close (gear-icon click, outside click, Escape key).

To add a fourth slider later (if a real music system or another audio channel shows up): add the <input type="range"> + label row to the .settings-panel markup, add a {el, val, get, set} entry to the sliders object in initSettingsPanel(), done — the open/close/sync plumbing is generic and doesn't need touching.

Speech framework's voice cue

SoundKit.voiceTone(seed) is a two-tone blip gated by voiceVolume (not sfxVolume), added specifically for the speech/dialogue framework (task list item 8). It takes a numeric seed — callers pass the speaking card's own uid — and derives a small pitch offset from it (340 + (seed%7)*55), so the same physical card instance sounds consistent across its own lifetime without any real voice recording or per-character audio assets. This is a deliberate placeholder for "a character's voice, represented by an sfx from a set list" — see context-vfx-animation.md's speech-bubble section for the visual half, and game-design-v30-addendum.md for why this shipped as a scaffold rather than a full content pass (real per-character personality needs the user's creative input, not just engineering).

tone() takes an optional final volOverride parameter specifically so voiceTone() can reuse the exact same oscillator-envelope code while gating off voiceVolume instead of sfxVolume — if a future audio channel needs the same treatment (a fourth independently-gated cue family), follow this same pattern rather than duplicating the oscillator logic.

Known gaps / open items

  • No actual music track or music-playing code exists — musicVolume is inert. Building this would need either a synthesized ambient loop (consistent with the no-external-files approach) or embedding a short audio file as a data: URI (size-budget tradeoff to weigh).

  • The speech framework's line content is generic placeholder chatter (see context-vfx-animation.md and game-design-v30-addendum.md) — no per-character voice identity beyond the deterministic pitch-by-uid trick.

  • voiceTone's pitch derivation is a simple modulo, not tuned for actually sounding distinct per character — worth revisiting if/when real character personalities get designed.

claude/sfx-cue-sheet.md read before: sound cue list

SFX cue sheet (draft, 2026-10-03)

Each cue lists what triggers it, its mood, and a target length. Today every cue is synthesized live in Web Audio inside SoundKit in arena_app.js. Each will be replaced by one or more short files in audio/, with 2–3 variations for anything that repeats a lot, so it doesn't sound robotic.

Formats: OGG plus an M4A fallback (or MP3), mono, 44.1 kHz, normalised to about −16 LUFS.

The A and B columns are the two directions in D17: A is cosy/organic, B is chiptune. Option C mixes them.

# Cue Trigger Mood (A, cosy/organic) Mood (B, chiptune) Length
1 UI click Any button Soft wooden tick Short square blip < 80 ms
2 UI hover Hover on big tiles Faint leaf brush Tiny high blip < 60 ms
3 UI back / close Close a modal Paper slide Down-blip < 150 ms
4 Tab / map pan Switch tab, pan world Page turn / soft whoosh Sweep 200–300 ms
5 Card draw Draw from deck Card slide off a stack Rising blip 150 ms
6 Card play Card lands on the board Card slap on felt, plus a wood thump Low thunk 200 ms
7 Card flip Pack reveal, flip Crisp paper flip Two-note flip 120 ms
8 Hit (light) Damage 1–3 Small thwack Noise burst 120 ms
9 Hit (heavy) Damage 4+ or crit Big thump with crack Low crunch 250 ms
10 Dodge Airborne/Illusory dodge Wing whoosh Pitch-bend swoosh 200 ms
11 Poison tick Poison damage Bubbling glop Wobbly blip 200 ms
12 Heal Heal Soft chime with a breath Arpeggio up 300 ms
13 Death Card dies Puff of leaves Descending blips 300 ms
14 Castle hit Castle damage Stone thud, dust Low boom 300 ms
15 Castle destroyed A castle at 0 Collapse rumble Long noise fall 1.2 s
16 Turn start Your turn Small bell Two-note "ready" 300 ms
17 Victory Win Warm fanfare (strings and pipe) 8-bit fanfare 2–3 s
18 Defeat Loss Low cello sigh Sad descending tune 2 s
19 Coin / reward Currency gained Leaf rustle with a coin clink Coin blip 250 ms
20 Pack shake Pack opening Rustling pouch Rattle 600 ms
21 Pack burst Pack opens Paper rip with a sparkle Burst 400 ms
22 Rare reveal Rare+ card flipped Shimmering chime Sparkle arpeggio 800 ms
23 Node select Pick a skirmish Pin into map Select blip 150 ms
24 Raid boss roar Raid fight start Deep sea-creature groan Distorted growl 1.5 s
25 Trench telegraph Column about to be smashed Rising creak and rumble Warning beeps 800 ms
26 Deny Illegal action Dull wood knock Buzz 150 ms
27 Level up Player level Bright bell flourish Level-up jingle 1.5 s
28 Bow loosed Arrow / Fire Arrow fires Plucked string and an air whoosh (fire adds crackle) Twang blip 220 ms
29 Arrow lands Arrow hits Wooden thunk with a short quiver Thud blip 120 ms
30 Bleed-out A card dies (non-fire) Low wet squelch, two drips (pitch by cause: poison lower, cold higher) Down-blip and two drips 550 ms
31 Burn-away A card burns (heat) Soft roar with crackles Noise hiss 700 ms
32 Castle falls Castle at 0 Long rumble, stones settling Long noise fall 1.4 s
33 Pitch A card is pitched for resources Wood tock and a bright two-note chime Coin blip 330 ms

Status (2026-10-05): cues 28–33 are live as Web Audio synths (SoundKit.arrowLoose/arrowThunk/bleedOut/burnAway/castleCollapse/pitchChime), built on two new primitives: filtered noise with a sweeping filter (fnoise) and a pitch-swept tone (sweep). They follow the "mixed" default for D17: an organic body with a little chip on top.

Status (2026-10-05, later):

  • Hits now use a richer synth: a thwack with random pitch, plus a boom and crack on heavy blows (SoundKit.hitAt).
  • Hits and deaths are panned to where they happen on screen (SoundKit.at).
  • New cues: 34 Materia forms (materiaForm, 800 ms, a glassy shimmer and pings) and 35 Hero level-up (heroLevel, 1.5 s, a rising bell arpeggio).
  • The ranked to-do list is in effects-catalogue.md → Recommendations.

Sources, CC0 first:

  • Kenney: Interface Sounds, Impact Sounds, RPG Audio, UI Audio, Casino Audio (card sounds).
  • OpenGameArt (CC0 filter).
  • Freesound (CC0 filter).
  • jsfxr for direction B and the UI blips.

Each file gets a line in CREDITS.md, even for CC0.

claude/pixellab-art-batch-status.md read before: card art (PixelLab)

PixelLab Art Batch — Status (updated 2026-10-03, ~17:20 SGT)

Read this (and style-guideline.md §9, art direction) before generating or wiring card art.

Where things stand

  • ~123 of 341 cards have art. Done in the new style:
    • All 16 Otter/Hummingbird faction cards.
    • The Wandering Traveller (elderly field mouse).
    • Regenerated Dolphin Knight / Bee Knight / Otter Centurion / Cobalt Talon Skirmisher / Mosswing Laborer / Sunthroat Courier.
    • Menacing Dominion Nestguard / Sunspire Envoy.
    • The five chromatic dragons.
    • Conquest maps 1–7: every creature in their decks (except the Bat Swarmling token).
    • Map 8 (2026-10-03 evening, 10 of 11): Bog Leech, Marsh Gas Toad, Swamp Alligator, Mire Witch-Heron, Cypress Root Lurker, Adder Ambusher, Venomlord Serpent, Constrictor Coil, Crocodile Ambusher, Wound Reaver.
    • Plus the splash (art/splash.png).
  • ⛔ PixelLab monthly generation limit reached (2026-10-03, ~17:10 SGT, mid-batch). The site offers "Buy credits" / "Upgrade plan". Nothing was bought; that's Jay's call.
  • Still to do (prompts are ready in art_staging/jobs_m8_10.json):
    • Map 8: Gangrenous Leech. The first try came out as lizards; use the explicit "NO legs, segmented tube body, sucker mouth" prompt queued in the runner.
    • Map 9: Cinder Hornet, Ember Jackal, Sulfur Vent Crab, Magma Salamander, Obsidian Scorpion, Basalt Boar, Magma Titan, Pyroclast Wyrm, Phoenix Fledgling.
    • Map 10: Mountain Goat Climber, Peak Condor, Golden Eagle Diver, Alpine Avalanche, Rockslide Ram, Eyrie Warden.
    • Home 2.5D sprites: home-otter, home-hummingbird, generated with Remove background ON. Save them as art/home/otter.png and art/home/hummingbird.png; the build embeds them automatically, and the Home parallax scene is already coded.
    • Then maps 11+ and cards outside Conquest decks.
  • Hall of Fame Classic drafts (Otter Centurion, Bee Knight) deliberately reuse the ORIGINAL painterly art from before the 2026-10-02 regeneration (recovered from git f8fb2d3^).
  • ~20 older portraits from 2026-09-18 are still in the earlier "painterly action" style — candidates for regeneration.
  • Lessons:
    • Abstract, swarm or worm-like subjects need very explicit anatomy in the prompt ("NO legs", body shape).
    • "Cute" + an eerie subject (Mire Witch-Heron) gives three cute blobs and one real heron. Pick by subject, or use Brave for creepy-but-small cards.
    • Always name a background scene, or PixelLab may return a flat grey backdrop.
    • If a job sits on "Generating…" for 15+ minutes (usually after the Mac slept), reload the tab, re-inject the helpers and resubmit — the stalled job is lost.
    • Speed this session: about 30 s to 2.5 min per image.
    • Before a big batch, check the remaining monthly generations; the limit shows as a "Generation Limit Reached" modal and jobs just never finish.

Genre prompt prefixes (see style guide §9 for when to use which)

  • ANAT (always appended for animals): natural animal anatomy, not anthropomorphic, no hands, never standing upright like a human,
  • Cute: Cute Pokemon-style pixel art creature portrait, big sparkling expressive eyes, soft round chubby shapes, bright cheerful palette, clean dark outlines, + ANAT
  • Brave: Pokemon-style pixel art creature portrait, confident determined eyes, sturdy rounded shapes, rich warm palette, dynamic action pose, clean dark outlines, + ANAT
  • Menacing: Pokemon-style pixel art portrait of a fearsome creature, sharp narrowed glowing eyes, angular silhouette, dramatic low-angle lighting, deep saturated shadowy palette, imposing pose, clean dark outlines, + ANAT (dragons: "natural dragon anatomy with four legs and two wings, not humanoid"; golems/trolls/elementals: no ANAT)

Then: <subject + pose>, <equipment>, <scene>, <background>. Rule of thumb by stats: Attack ≤2 and Health ≤10 → Cute; Attack ≥6 or Health ≥24 → Menacing; otherwise Brave.

Tool + settings

  • https://www.pixellab.ai/create?tool=create_image_pro (signed in, in the desktop app's built-in browser). Pro = 20 generations per image.
  • Output size select index 2 = 128×128, which returns a 256×256 sheet of FOUR variations (crop the best quadrant). Index 4 = 344×192 (16:9, single image — splash).
  • Background removal OFF for card art; ON for the Home 2.5D sprites.

Technique (no downloads, no permission prompts)

  • Helpers in the PixelLab tab (re-inject after any reload; full source in art_staging/inject_m8_10.js):
    • window.__bwGen(prompt, sizeIdx, removeBg) — set the textarea and the size select via the native HTMLTextAreaElement/HTMLSelectElement value setters, dispatching input/change, then set the "Remove background" checkbox to removeBg.
    • window.__bwRun2(jobs, sizeIdx) — polls up to 15 minutes per job; ids starting home- get background removal on. Appending to window.__bwJobs2 while it runs queues more jobs.
    • Results go to window.__bwArt[cardId]; progress, with timestamps, to window.__bwLog.
  • Transfer: javascript_tool returns 'BWART_JSON::'+JSON.stringify({id: dataURI,...}) (3–4 images per call). The oversized result is saved to a tool-results file; art_staging/extract.py <file> writes art_staging/raw/<id>.png.
  • Wiring:
    1. art_staging/sheet.py out.png ids… builds a labelled A–D contact sheet for picking.
    2. Record picks in art_staging/picks.json.
    3. Run art_staging/crop.py, filtered to the new ids (it re-crops every pick otherwise). It writes art/cards/<id>.png.
    4. art_staging/wire_art.py writes the data URIs into canonical/cards.json.
    5. python3 assemble_arena.py, then mv bramblewood-arena.html index.html, commit; Vercel auto-deploys.

claude/testing-strategy.md read before: testing

Bramblewood Arena — Testing Strategy (2026-10-03)

How to test card-vs-card interactions, two-player interactions, and multi-player offline modes (Async Arena, Raid). The aim is auto setup and auto test. Everything below is built and runs today:

tests/run-all.sh          # ~15 s, engine level, no browser
tests/run-all.sh --e2e    # + two real browser pages playing a live match (~1 min)

All suites use the real engine (bramblewood-engine.js) and real card data (canonical/cards.json). Everything is seeded, so the same inputs always give the same fight. That determinism is what makes snapshot (golden) testing work.

The test pyramid

Layer What it proves Speed File
Card-vs-card matrix No card pairing crashes, breaks an invariant, or behaves non-deterministically; any change in how two cards interact shows up as a diff 4 s sampled, 16 s full tests/card-matrix.js
Mechanic scenarios Every card's effects actually do something; core rules hold exactly 1 s tests/scenarios.js + tests/scenarios/*.json
Two-player (engine) Two independent seats: legality, fairness, replayability 3 s tests/two-player.js
Multi-player offline Async Arena ghost pools per win stage; Raid party and shared pool 3 s tests/async-raid.js
Two-browser live match The real host/peer code paths over a relay ~1 min tests/e2e/live_two_player.py
User-story walk The 54 stories in user-stories.md, in a real browser manual or agent run Playwright scripts

1. Card-vs-card interactions (auto setup + auto test)

Auto setup. There are no hand-written fixtures. Pairings are generated from the card list:

  • Duels: every fieldable card vs every other (--full, 77k fights), or each card vs 40 others (default).
  • Squads: 3v3 boards across lanes and both battle modes, which covers Guardian, Sweep, Rally and area effects.
  • Matches: full 20-card matches, with the engine AI on both seats.

Seeds come from the card ids, so a new card gets its fights automatically.

Auto test, three layers:

  1. Invariants after every round. No NaN or negative HP or attack. No dead card left on the board. No duplicate uids. No negative resources. No exceptions.
  2. Determinism. A sample is replayed with the same seed and must match exactly.
  3. Golden signatures. Each fight's winner, rounds, final HPs, event histogram and an event-order hash are stored in tests/golden/card-matrix.json. After any engine or card change, the run lists every fight that changed. Expected changes are accepted with --update; unexpected ones are bugs.

Mechanic scenarios.

  • Auto "every effect does something". Each card with effects is fought as-is and with its effects stripped, in six small contexts (attacker, blocker, 3-wide line, each also with ×5 health). If nothing differs, the effect is dead data. The core keywords (poison, thorns, flying, guardian, sweep and others) fail the run; the rest are listed for review.
  • Hand-written JSON scenarios. Exact setups with expectations (event counts with field filters, winner, castle HP bounds, cards alive). Writing one is about five lines, and the format is documented at the top of scenarios.js. Fifteen exist today: core rules plus Rend, Grit, On Kill, and Swap.

Results today.

  • 0 failures across 81k fights.
  • 155 sampled stalemates (957 in the full run) are listed for design review. They're mostly armor walls (Barnacle Fortress, Termite Mound, Stalactite Golem) that low-attack cards can never get through.
  • 4 effects have no scenario yet: King Maker (kingSlayer needs a King on the board), Nest, Pack Rat Looter, and Wandering Traveller.

2. Two-player interactions

Engine level (two-player.js). Each seat is driven by its own scripted policy, not the shared AI: greedy, tempo, random, and a cheater that tries to play twice in one turn. Both seats plan, then one combat resolves, the same order used by Pass & Play, Live Ranked and friend invites.

  • Every policy pairing: invariants hold, the hand never exceeds 5, and illegal actions are refused.
  • Seat fairness: mirror decks and mirror policies over 400 matches; seat 1 must win 42–58% of the decided matches. Today it's 48.5%.
  • Replay: re-running a match from its recorded actions on a fresh engine gives the identical end state. Live Ranked depends on this, because the host replays the peer's intents.

Browser level (e2e/live_two_player.py). Two separate browser contexts, so two players with separate storage, both run the real index.html. Supabase is replaced by e2e/mock-relay.js, and the test script relays realtime broadcasts page to page, so the real code paths run unmodified (enterLiveMatch, intents, state snapshots, abandon). It checks:

  • seat and role assignment;
  • after every round, the peer's mirror equals the host's state (round, both castles, both boards);
  • both pages agree on the winner;
  • a peer leaving tells the host.

Next steps for two-player:

  • Run the same script against a Supabase branch database to test the real RPCs (find_ranked_match, the friend-invite RPCs once the social migration is applied).
  • Add a "slow network" mode to the relay (delayed or reordered messages) to test races between an intent and a state broadcast.

3. Multi-player offline: Async Arena

Model (bramblewood-ghosts.js, shared by the game and the tests):

  • A run is up to 7 wins and ends at 3 losses.
  • Your win stage is your wins so far in the run.
  • At each stage you fight a ghost: a snapshot of a deck that also won at that stage, played by the AI.
  • Each stage needs at least 12 active decks. Active means recorded in the last 14 days, the newest deck per player, and a legal deck.
  • Real recorded decks are topped up with seeded ghost decks generated for that stage. Seeded decks are drawn from a power band that rises with the stage and rotate weekly.
  • Your own winning deck is recorded at the stage where you won.

Auto setup (population sim). 40 synthetic players play 80 full runs headlessly and record their winning decks, exactly as real players would. After the sim, every stage has a real pool:

Stage 0 1 2 3 4 5 6
Real decks 36 31 25 19 15 13 9
Seeded top-up 0 0 0 0 0 0 3

That shows how pools fill naturally, and that the top-up only kicks in where real decks are thin.

Auto test.

  • Every stage has ≥ 10 (target 12) legal decks with no duplicate owners.
  • Ghost strength rises with the stage. In real fights, top-stage ghosts beat bottom-stage ghosts 92% of the time; the check requires ≥ 65%.
  • Stale, illegal, other-stage and duplicate-owner decks never leak into a pool.
  • A player never meets the same ghost twice in a run.
  • Every run ends correctly.

Today the pool is local (this browser's recorded decks plus seeds). To share real decks between players, add a cloud async_ghosts table (owner, stage, deck, recorded_at), write to it on each win, and read the newest per owner per stage. The rules and the tests stay the same.

4. Multi-player offline: Raid

Model:

  • One featured boss per week, rotating through the 13 bosses in canonical/raid-bosses.json.
  • The boss has a shared HP pool (its HP × 8).
  • Loading the raid pulls in the decks that already fought it this week, with their real damage. For now these are your recorded attempts; the online raid_attempts.deck_snapshot rows can feed in the same way.
  • Eight seeded stand-in raiders also fight, simulated with the real engine and together capped at 50% of the pool.
  • Stand-ins are fixed for the week, so the pool only ever goes down.
  • Your fight is against a boss castle of min(remaining, boss HP); your damage comes off the pool, and your deck joins the party.

Auto test.

  • Weekly rotation works, and every boss deck uses real cards.
  • Raid state is deterministic.
  • Pool math: max − total damage = remaining.
  • This week's decks for this boss only appear in the party.
  • A real attempt lowers the pool by exactly its damage.
  • The pool is monotonic across 12 added attempts.
  • Stand-ins never take more than half the pool, for every boss.

5. User-story walk

user-stories.md lists 54 stories with acceptance criteria. Walking them was done with Playwright (scripts in the session scratch). The plan is to turn the most valuable stories into a permanent tests/e2e/stories.py, one function per story. Candidates:

  • US-02 tutorial
  • US-07 resume
  • US-13 quit everywhere
  • US-17 to 21 Conquest loop
  • US-25 Async run
  • US-29 and US-30 Raid
  • US-32 deck gate
  • US-48 no horizontal scroll

6. When to run what

When Run
Every engine or card change tests/run-all.sh. Golden diffs show exactly which interactions changed.
Before deploy tests/run-all.sh --e2e
After balance work node tests/card-matrix.js --full, then review stalemates and diffs
CI (future) A GitHub Action running tests/run-all.sh on every push (needs Node only; --e2e needs Playwright and Chromium)

claude/i18n.md read before: translations

Languages (2026-10-05)

Status: the translation layer is built. The game's main frame (about 130 strings) is translated into 12 languages. Everything else still shows in English until it's added to the packs.

Languages

English (source) plus:

Code Language Draft quality
fr Français Close to final
de Deutsch Close to final
es Español Close to final
it Italiano Close to final
pt Português (European) Close to final
id Bahasa Indonesia (chosen over Malay: far more speakers) Close to final
zh-Hans 简体中文 Close to final
zh-Hant 繁體中文 Close to final
tl Tagalog Have a native speaker read it
ta தமிழ் Have a native speaker read it
ja 日本語 Have a native speaker read it
ko 한국어 Have a native speaker read it

Players pick the language in Settings → 🌐 Language. The choice is remembered on their device.

How it works

The code is in bramblewood-i18n.js.

  • Page translator. While a non-English language is active, it watches the page. It swaps any text, title, label or placeholder whose English matches a pack entry. It ignores leading emoji and symbols, and keeps them.
    • Buttons and labels translate without anyone touching the code that draws them.
    • Switching back to English restores the originals.
  • Not translated: card names (.nm), anything inside translate="no", inputs and text areas.
  • i18('Need {n} more 🍁', {n}) in code: for sentences with numbers or names in them. The page translator can't match those, because the English varies.
  • Packs: lang/<code>.json holds {"English": "translation"}.
    • The build turns each pack into assets/lang/<code>.js, loaded only when that language is picked.
    • With BW_INLINE=1 they're built into the page instead.
  • Fonts: Tamil gets Baloo Thambi 2, the Tamil sister of our Baloo font. Chinese, Japanese and Korean get Noto Sans in that script. Each loads from Google Fonts only when its language is picked, and CJK arrives in small slices.
  • Missing strings: BramblewoodI18n.misses() in the browser console lists every English string seen on screen with no translation yet. Use it as the to-do list.

Adding or fixing translations

  1. Add a row to tools/i18n_core.py, or edit lang/<code>.json directly. Hand edits survive re-running the script.
  2. Run python3 tools/i18n_core.py, then the normal build.
  3. Run python3 tests/e2e/i18n.py. It checks every pack loads, translates and switches back.

Still English (next passes)

Area Why it isn't done yet Approach
Long help sentences, tooltips, coach tips, dialogue Lots of text; waiting for wording to settle Harvest with misses(), translate in batches
Sentences with numbers ("Needs 2🪵 — you have 0🪵", "3 cards", "0/4 cleared") The English varies, so the page translator can't match them Wrap them in i18() with {n}, then translate
Skill descriptions (124, built from templates) They're generated in code Give each template its own i18() sentence
Card names and flavour (343) Your call: translate or keep English? Translating would need per-language card files
Map, skirmish and rival names, trash talk Waiting on the lore-bible renames After the renames
Text-length layout pass German, French and Tamil run long Check the tight spots (card scrolls, chips) per language

claude/lore-bible.md read before: lore, names, rival lines

Bramblewood lore bible (2026-10-05)

What this is: the reference for who lives in Bramblewood, how they look and how they fight. Use it when generating art, designing skirmishes or writing cards and their lines. It supersedes the tone of lore-otters-vs-hummingbirds.md where they differ. The Crown of Currents plot, Whisper-of-Ash and the Twin Bloom still stand; the Otter and Hummingbird cultures are re-cast below.

The world in one paragraph

Almost every animal in Bramblewood can talk, but talking doesn't make it civilised. It's still an animal-eat-animal world: most creatures live by tooth, claw and appetite, and only a few have built anything like a society. The two that have built the most are the Otters, a disciplined river legion, and the Hummingbirds, a loose federation of brightly painted tribes. They've gone to war. Everyone else is caught between them:

  • the mice and rats who wander the roads;
  • the other mammals who sell to, mend and feed both armies;
  • the beasts, who mostly just want to eat.

The peoples

Otters: the Rivergate Legion

Culture: regimented, pre-Roman-empire. A young republic becoming an empire:

  • Built: roads, river forts, bridges and stone-and-timber camps.
  • Ranked: centuries, centurions, standard-bearers, a Marshal.
  • Bound together by oath and drill.

They win by formation, not ferocity. They tame and train fish as companions and mounts, the way Rome had war-dogs and cavalry: pike lancers, carp couriers, trout shield-mates.

Look:

  • Bronze and iron; red-brown wool tunics; rectangular shields with a wave or fish emblem.
  • Crested helmets; standards topped with a bronze fish.
  • Neat ranks; river forts with palisades.
  • Palette: bronze, oxblood red, river blue, cream.

On the board:

  • Guardian, Armor and shield-wall effects; Rally and formation bonuses.
  • Discipline: steady stats, Wait 0–1.
  • Fish companions that spawn or fight beside them.
  • Few tricks.

Voice: clipped and formal, like military orders. "Shields up. Hold the line. Advance on my mark."

Hummingbirds: the Sunfeather Tribes

Culture: tribal, proud and loud with colour. Many tribes, each named for its feather colour: Crimson Wing, Cobalt Talon, Gold-throat, Mosswing, Violet Vane, Voidfeather, Sunthroat. They're bound by kinship, song and the Twin Bloom gatherings, not by an emperor. Their elders lead by voice and deed.

They are friends with the large beasts: the great stags, bears, boars and big cats will fight beside a tribe that sings to them. That's why a Hummingbird war-band can field things far bigger than itself.

Look:

  • War-paint and feather headdresses; beads, totems, woven banners.
  • Flower garlands, nests woven into cliffs.
  • Movement everywhere, very bright.
  • Palette: saturated jewel tones (crimson, cobalt, gold, violet, emerald) on warm cliff stone.

On the board:

  • Speed: Flying, Evasive, Swift, Quick.
  • Colour or tribe synergies; songs and blessings as buffs.
  • Beast-bond: large beasts get bonuses alongside Hummingbirds.
  • Glass-cannon attackers.

Voice: chant-like and boastful, nature-flavoured. "The Bloom sings for us today."

Mice and rats: the road-folk

Culture: travellers and nomads. Caravans, peddlers, smugglers, rumour-sellers, scavengers and the occasional rat-king of a road gang. They belong to no side and sell to both. The Wandering Traveller in the shop is one of them.

Look:

  • Patched cloaks; oversized packs and bedrolls; lanterns and walking sticks.
  • Carts pulled by beetles; trinkets everywhere.
  • Palette: dusty browns, faded indigo, lantern amber.

On the board:

  • Economy and theft: pitch bonuses, stealing resources, Bounty.
  • Moving and swapping cards; scavenging from the graveyard; cheap fodder.

Voice: haggling, sly, chatty. "Everything's for sale, friend. Even advice. Especially advice."

Other mammals: the supporting folk

Who: badgers, moles, beavers, rabbits, chipmunks, deer folk, sloths, porcupines, turtles and the like. They're the craftsmen, builders, farmers, smiths, healers, ferrymen and shopkeepers who keep both armies fed and housed.

  • Some are hired muscle (badger sappers, rabbit scouts).
  • Most just want the war to go somewhere else.
  • Beavers build for the Legion.
  • Chipmunks stockpile and sell.

On the board:

  • Support: heal, buff, build structures; generate Lumber or Stone.
  • Defensive bodies; reliable, middling stats.
  • Mercenary versions exist (Badger Berserker), but they fight for pay, not a flag.

Voice: practical, folksy, a bit weary. "Another army through the turnips. Lovely."

Carnivores: the beasts

Who: wolves, foxes, wild cats, bears, snakes, crocodiles and monitors, scorpions and spiders, and raptors when they hunt.

  • They talk, but they don't negotiate. They're hunger with a vocabulary.
  • They form packs, not nations.
  • The big ones can be befriended by Hummingbird song. Otters fight them, or wall them off.

Look:

  • Lean and scarred; glowing eyes in the dark.
  • Lairs, bones and claw marks.
  • Palette: shadowed greens and browns with a single hot accent (eye glow, blood, ember).

On the board:

  • High attack; Frenzy, Bleed, Ambush, Pack bonuses.
  • Little or no support text; "feral" means no leader synergies.
  • Big ones are natural Hummingbird beast-bond partners.

Voice: short, hungry, menacing. "You smell like supper."

Dogs and cats: friends of both (decided 2026-10-08)

Dogs and cats are neutral creatures, not pets and not beasts. They're friends of both peoples: a hound might run messages for an Otter cohort one season and guard a Hummingbird camp the next. They choose whom they help.

  • War dogs fight alongside whoever they've sworn to. They stay four-legged: no armour-wearing biped knights. They carry their weapon in their mouth (a short blade, a spiked club, a torch), and wear at most a collar, a harness or a light barding.
  • Cats are the same: independent allies, quick and proud, never owned. Big wild cats (lynx, leopard) remain beasts (see Carnivores); house-sized cats are the neutral kind.
  • On the board: neutral cards both decks can use; loyalty and escort effects (guard an ally, follow a leader), not pack-hunting Frenzy.
  • Art: quadrupeds, weapon held in the jaws, practical straps; no human-style armour suits.
  • Voice: warm, loyal, a little cheeky. "I go where the friendly smell goes."

The few societies

Only a handful of creatures live in true societies: ants, bees, termites and other hives. They're collective and tireless, with a strange sort of fairness, all for the hive. They're neutral powers with their own territory (the hills, the hives).

On the board: spawning, swarming, sharing (colony and nest effects).

Voice: "We." Never "I."

Fish and other water folk

  • Most fish don't speak much and are tamed by the Otters as companions.
  • The deep things (kraken, cuttlefish, whales) are old, strange and independent. Raid bosses live here.

Subfactions to grow into (sketch, 2026-10-12)

You said we'll build these out as we go. These are starting points to react to; nothing here is decided.

Subfaction Who they might be Where they live Board identity (fits a skill we have)
The Ashen Choir (cultists) Moths, beetles and a few lost Hummingbirds who worship the dragons' old fire The Ashen Peak, the Basalt Foundry Devilry and Ritual: they sacrifice their own to call something bigger
The Greenhood Band (Robin Hood raiders) Foxes, magpies and outcast mice who rob the toll roads and share it out The Briar Maze, the Redwood Deep Swipe (stealing Lumber), Stealth, Backstab
The Hollow Kin (cave dwellers) Moles, bats, blind salamanders; the tiny cave voice from Sunken Hollow is one of theirs Caves & Alcoves, Smugglers' Grotto Converge (pulling a line together in the dark), Blind, Lantern
The Gnawed (cannibals) Rats and shrews who stopped caring what they eat; the road-folk's dark mirror The Sable Swampmire, the Glass Dunes Devour, Sap, Pack (they gather and grow)
The Tidefolk (mer-people) Otters who never came ashore, eels, seals: half-legend even to the Legion Coral Current, the Kelp Mangroves Tide, Wash, Fork

The dragons. The Dragon Scales on the road hint that dragons were here before the border war. That's the thread to pull for:

  • the world boss on Map 20;
  • what the Ashen Choir prays to;
  • why castles can break their limits with Dragon Scales.

Open questions for you:

  • Are the dragons gone, sleeping or hiding?
  • Is anyone descended from them?

Using this

For art (PixelLab or image prompts)

Start every prompt with the people's look line above, then the subject. Examples:

  • "Pixel art, pre-Roman otter legionary, bronze crested helmet, oxblood tunic, rectangular wave-emblem shield, river fort behind, bronze/oxblood/river-blue palette."
  • "Pixel art, hummingbird tribal warrior, crimson war-paint, feather headdress, bead necklace, flower garland, cliff nests, saturated jewel tones."
  • "Pixel art, mouse peddler, patched cloak, huge travelling pack with pots and lanterns, dusty road, lantern-amber light."
  • "Pixel art, feral wolf, lean and scarred, glowing eyes, dark forest, single ember accent."

Map backgrounds:

  • Otter territory gets roads, forts and bridges.
  • Hummingbird territory gets painted cliffs, totems and blossoms.
  • Beast territory gets wild ground, lairs and bones.
  • Road-folk get caravan camps.

For skirmishes

Every skirmish should say who you're fighting, and the deck should match:

  • Otter Legion patrols: disciplined decks (Otters, fish, Guardian). Lines are orders.
  • Hummingbird war-bands: fast decks (Hummingbirds plus a big beast). Lines are boasts.
  • Beast hunts: wild decks (one predator type, a pack). Lines are hunger.
  • Road-folk ambushes: cheap thieving decks (mice and rats). Lines are haggling.
  • Hive defences: swarm decks (ants, bees). Lines are "we".

Bosses are named characters from the plot where possible: Shieldback, Vesper, Aurelia, Whisper-of-Ash.

For card design

  • New cards get one people (above). Their keywords should come from that people's "On the board" list.
  • Carnivores should not get support text (heal, resource generation).
  • Mice and rats should not get heavy armour.
  • Otter cards should not be fast and flimsy; Hummingbird cards should not be slow walls.

Known mismatches in the current roster (proposals, nothing renamed yet)

Card or thing Why it doesn't fit Suggestion
Dominion Nestguard; "Sunfeather Dominion" in the old doc "Dominion" reads as an empire; the Hummingbirds are tribes now "Sunfeather Nestguard"; call the faction "the Sunfeather Tribes"
The old caste system (rank by colour) Tribal, not a caste hierarchy Keep the colours as tribe names (Crimson Wing tribe, etc.)
Stag Knight, Bee Knight, Dolphin Knight, Cataphract Destrier Medieval knights don't fit a pre-Roman world Stag → "Stag Champion" (beast-bonded); Bee → "Bee Warden"; Dolphin → "Dolphin Outrider"; Cataphract → "War Destrier"
War Hound, Armored Mastiff, Shepherd's Bark ✅ Settled 2026-10-08 Neutral friends of both peoples (see "Dogs and cats"); war dogs carry weapons in their mouths and stay four-legged. Art passes should drop any biped/armoured-knight look.
Raccoon Warlord, Raccoon Bandit Raccoons aren't covered by your list Treat raccoons as road-folk (bandits and thieves), alongside rats
Rodent King Fits as a rat road-gang boss Keep; flavour as "king of the road"

Answered: dogs (and house cats) are neutral friends of both peoples. Still open: are the beasts that befriend the Hummingbirds only the big ones (stags, bears, big cats, boars), or any beast?

D20. Devilry: my readings of your list (all built, all revertible) · Decide · Design · asked 2026-10-10

  • Built as I read them: (1) Dark Summon is a second play each turn, just for ⛧ cards, and every Dark Summon card also costs Darkness (a free extra play measured at 100%). (2) Darkness comes from your own units perishing (+1 each); 'dark actions' were left open. (3) Devour uses your play for the turn and works once per devourer. (4) Ritual's 'units perished' counts both sides. (5) Sacrifice N sits on the fodder; the discount comes off Darkness first, then Lumber. (6) Your blank keyword is named Offering N. 2026-10-10 (you: 'darkness is not clear. Is it gained? idk'): yes, it is gained: +1 each time one of YOUR units perishes (Ash Imp adds +1 on arrival). It's shown only if your deck has at least one Devilry card: the pill reads '★ 3 · ⛧ 1' (Darkness held, dark summons left this turn), and each gain floats a ★ +1. You gain 1 dark summon per turn; it doesn't stack.
    • Keep all six
    • Change some (tell me which)
  • Default: Keep all six
